## Reasoning

**Champion analysis:** The new champion (score 12621.61) is a 2-state machine:
- State 0: GoToColor(blue=3), transitions on: black floor (p=0.8) → state1, neighbors<3 (w=10) → state1
- State 1: Repulsion (rep=3.5), transitions on: perceive blue (p=0.7) → state0, gray floor (p=0.3) → state0

This makes sense: robots go toward blue walls (target strip), once on black floor they repulse each other to spread/aggregate in the strip, and return to seeking blue if they see it again or if on gray.

The gap between rank 1 (12621) and rank 2 (17672) is huge - the champion is clearly superior. Focus exploitation on it.

**Key observations:**
- GoToColor(blue) + Repulsion is the winning pattern
- Transitions: black floor detection keeps robots in the strip, neighbor count triggers repulsion
- The 3-state variants (ranks 2-5) with Spiral+Repulsion+Exploration score ~17k-18k

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion exactly (required)
2. Exploit champion: tune rep gain, probabilities, thresholds
3. Try 3-state extensions of the champion pattern
4. Explore: GoToColor(blue) + different secondary behaviors

For 2-state machines, leaving state 0: others=[1], D=0→state1. Leaving state 1: others=[0], D=0→state0.

Let me design carefully:

**Exploitation variants of champion:**
- M2: rep=4.0, p(black)=0.85, p(blue)=0.75
- M3: rep=3.0, neighbors threshold=2, p(gray)=0.4
- M4: rep=3.5, add gray floor transition from state0
- M5: rep=4.5, p(black)=0.7, w=12
- M6: 3-state: GoToColor(blue) → Repulsion → Exploration

**Exploration:**
- M7: 3-state with GoToColor + Stop + Repulsion
- M8: 2-state GoToColor(blue) + Attraction (aggregate once there)
- M9: 3-state GoToColor + Repulsion + GoToColor variant
- M10: 4-state complex
- M11: 2-state variant with different transition mix
- M12: 3-state from rank6 variant
- M13: 2-state with exploration instead of repulsion
- M14: 3-state GoToColor + Repulsion + Exploration (different params)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion analysis (score 6067.95):**
- State 0: GoToColor(blue=3), transitions: black floor (p=0.8)→state1, neighbors>5 with w=8 (C=3)→state1
- State 1: Repulsion (rep=3.5), transitions: perceive blue (p=0.7)→state0, black floor (p=0.5)→state0

Key insight: C=3 means neighbors ABOVE threshold. So `--c0x1 3 --p0x1 5 --w0x1 8` means "if neighbors above 5 (with steepness 8), go to repulsion state." This makes robots switch to repulsion when crowded, then return to GoToColor when they see blue or are on black floor.

**Rank 2 (6779.50)** is also very strong - 3-state version with an extra GoToColor state for robots that are on gray floor.

**Large gap:** Ranks 1-2 (6k-6.7k) vs ranks 3+ (9.6k+). Focus heavily on exploiting the top 2.

**Key parameters to tune:**
- Champion: p(black)=0.8, neighbor_threshold=5, w=8, rep=3.5, p(blue)=0.7, p(black_from_s1)=0.5
- Try varying: threshold 4-6, w 6-10, rep 3.0-4.5, probabilities

**Strategy:**
1. Champion (exact copy)
2. Rank-2 exact copy (very strong)
3-8. Exploit champion: tune threshold, rep, probabilities
9-11. Exploit rank-2: tune its parameters
12-14. Explore: different topologies

For 2-state machines: leaving state 0, D=0→state1; leaving state 1, D=0→state0.
For 3-state machines: leaving state 0: D=0→state1, D=1→state2; leaving state 1: D=0→state0, D=1→state2; leaving state 2: D=0→state0, D=1→state1.

## Reasoning

**Situation:** The scores are converging tightly - ranks 1-11 are all between 3909 and 3992, a very small spread. The champion (3909) uses:
- State 0: GoToColor(blue=3), black_floor(p=0.8)→s1, neighbors>4 w=9 (C=3, threshold=4)→s1
- State 1: Repulsion(3.5), perceive_blue(p=0.7)→s0, neighbors<2 w=10 (C=4)→s0

Key: threshold=4 (not 5) with w=9 is slightly better than threshold=5 w=8.

**What I observe:**
- Almost all top machines use the same 2-state GoToColor+Repulsion structure
- Parameters are very close to optimal - we're in a very narrow basin
- The 3-state variant (rank 2, 3926) is almost as good
- Rank 9 (3951): uses black_floor instead of blue perception in s1 - also competitive

**Fine-tuning strategy:**
- Try threshold=3, w=9-11 in state0
- Try threshold=4, w=7-10 
- Try rep=3.2-3.8
- Try p(blue)=0.65-0.8 in state1
- Try p(black)=0.75-0.9 in state0
- Mix different transition types in state1

**Important note on error:** "Transition '--n1x1' is missing its destination index" - I must make sure every `--n<I>x<K>` has a corresponding destination. Looking at my format: I write `--n1x0 0 --c1x0 ...` which includes the destination. The error seems to be about `--n1 T` being confused with `--n1x1`. I need to ensure every `--nIxK D` is present.

Wait - re-reading the format: `--n<I>x<K> D` is the destination. So `--n1x0 0` means transition K=0 from state 1 goes to destination D=0. That's correct. The error message says `--n1x1` is missing destination - so I must have had `--n1 2` (number of transitions=2) but then only wrote one transition detail. Let me be very careful.

Actually looking at the error more carefully - it seems to be a persistent issue. Let me check my format carefully. The issue might be that I'm writing something like `--n1 2` and then transitions, but one transition is malformed. I'll be very explicit.

- threshold=3 for C=3 (rank 11, 4233) is worse than threshold=4
- Multiple machines tied at 4066.88 - suggesting stochastic noise is large relative to differences

**The champion's stochastic noise:** The same machine appears multiple times with score 4066.88 in round 8, while the champion got 3907.33 in round 6. This suggests ~5% variance from randomness. The true optimum might not be far from 3900.

**Strategy for final rounds:**
1. Keep champion
2. Try even finer perturbations: p(black)=0.74, 0.76, 0.71
3. Try different rep values: 3.45, 3.55
4. Try w=8.5, w=9.0 (already optimal), w=9.2
5. Try p(blue)=0.68, 0.71 in state1
6. Explore genuinely different topologies in case there's a better basin:
   - GoToColor + FlockCenter
   - GoToColor + Stop (with smart transitions)
   - 3-state with attraction as middle state
7. Try C=3 threshold=4 but different w in state0

**Note on persistent error:** The error says `--n1x1` missing destination. I write `--n1x1 0` which includes destination. Perhaps the error occurred in machines where I had `--n1 2` but then the second transition was `--n1x1` without destination D explicitly stated. I'll be very careful to always write `--nIxK D` with D present.

## Reasoning

**Major breakthrough!** The champion jumped from 3907 to 3108 by replacing Repulsion with **FlockCenter** (B=10, fc=30) in state 1! 

Champion: GoToColor(blue) → FlockCenter(fc=30), with transitions: perceive_blue(p=0.7)→s0, neighbors<2 w=10→s0

This makes perfect sense: robots go toward blue walls, then once on black floor they **flock together** (move toward group center) rather than repulse. Flocking keeps them aggregated in the target strip.

**Key parameters to tune for FlockCenter:**
- fc=30: try 20, 25, 35, 40, 50
- p(blue)=0.7 in s1: try 0.65, 0.75
- threshold=2, w=10 for C=4 in s1: try threshold=1,3 and w=8,12
- p(black)=0.75 in s0: try 0.70, 0.80
- threshold=4, w=9 for C=3 in s0: try 3,5 and w=8,10

**Exploration:** 
- Try Attraction (B=4) instead of FlockCenter
- Try 3-state with FlockCenter
- Try different fc values more aggressively

This is the last round, so maximize coverage of the FlockCenter basin while keeping champion.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
